theme/primitives.css carries at least one infinite animation. A pixel baseline needs frozen animation, pinned fonts and a fixed renderer before it can be asserted across machines; speed and reliability pull against each other there.
Playwright's toHaveScreenshot costs no new dependency, keeps baselines in-repo and supports per-platform snapshot suffixes, but makes every developer machine a baseline authority.
A dedicated visual-diff service normalizes across platforms and brings a review UI, but is a hosted tier that CLAUDE.md's fully-open-source rule constrains hard.
The visual regression tool and approach are not decided in the roadmap; P6's own Opus planning pass picks one, together with containment measures and which surfaces get baselines.
P6 is scheduled last so it baselines a tree P1-P5 have already changed rather than one they would immediately invalidate.
P1 is P6's real precondition: a pixel tier's affordable size depends on what the suites already cost, and P1 is the phase that measures that.

### v1.4 P7 Git graph polish: row/bullet geometry, uncommitted-work click-through, file-tree icon colors
Every commit row in the git graph (packages/git-ui) has a fixed 36px height, set by --kv-row-height in theme/density.css as a single SlickGrid-wide option.
CommitGrid.vue's .kv-cell-message CSS grid unconditionally reserves both the 16px ref/PR-badge track and the 18px subject track, so undecorated commits get the same height as decorated ones.
The graph node's y-coordinate in rowSvg.ts (planNode/edgeCommand) is cy = rowHeight / 2, centering on the fixed row total rather than the subject line.
Target design for the graph: only a row with a ref/PR badge should be two lines tall, and every row's bullet should align to its own commit-message line rather than the row midpoint.
The installed SlickGrid fork (5.20.0) ships enableVariableRowHeight/ItemMetadata.height, unused in this repo (getItemMetadata returns only cssClasses), and density.css has an unused --kv-row-height-compact: 20px token; P7 builds on these.
UncommittedChangesStrip.vue is a pinned, deliberately non-interactive strip above the grid (role="status", no click handler).
A GitLens-style pseudo-row for uncommitted changes inside the graph was rejected in v1 because it would need a ~25-site grid-row-index-to-store-row-index remapping; this constraint still holds.
A click action on the uncommitted strip was deferred in v1 because it needed a sha-shaped IPC method, which "uncommitted" lacks; P7 makes the strip focusable and clickable and adds the IPC method working.detail.
working.detail composes git status's untracked entries with a plain git diff --numstat/--name-status HEAD into the same FileChange[] shape commit.detail produces; it must never use diff-tree, which cannot reach the working tree.
Because working.detail returns the commit.detail FileChange[] shape, the existing FileTree.vue rendering needs no new component logic, only a new pane variant and a third selection state alongside commit/stash.
FileTree.vue's setiIconFor (icons/setiFileIcon.ts) wraps the seti-icons@0.0.4 npm package, whose bundled definitions.json is a stale upstream snapshot.
The stale seti package renders .test.ts/.spec.ts/.test.tsx/.spec.tsx yellow (#cbcb41), whereas VS Code's seti theme (vs-seti-icon-theme.json) renders them orange (#e37933); this is the color fix.
VS Code maps .spec.cjs/.test.cjs/.spec.mjs/.test.mjs to seti icons but the stale seti-icons package has no entry for them; all other mappings already match VS Code.
The three P7 items (row geometry, uncommitted click-through, seti icon colors) are independent and bundled only because they came from the same graph-surface review pass.
<!-- /chapter v1.4 -->

<!-- chapter v1.5 -->
## v1.5
Source: docs/v1.5/ at bd0c224688d9. 14 phases.
v1.5 is the code intelligence chapter: a fourth top-level subsystem beside studio, api and the headless git module, providing a native, read-only, VS Code-like workspace per imported repository.
The v1.5 workspace includes a filesystem project tree, tabs with preview/permanent/pinned behavior, Monaco-backed file/diff viewing, in-file and repository-wide Go-native search, quick-open, and tree-sitter-derived go-to-definition/implementation.
The v1.3 git graph is mounted as the workspace's own pinned first tab instead of living only in the separately-installed VS Code extension.
v1.5 includes a local MCP server that exposes the same code graph to an AI client.
The graph's code-review layer (inline AI-feedback gutter icons, comment threads over a diff) is deferred to the chapter's last phase, and its placement in the workspace is resolved then.
The v1.5 chapter uses a fresh phase prefix (C1, C2, …) rather than continuing v1.4's P numbering, because like v1.3's git chapter it is one cohesive subsystem; C stands for code.
v1.4 shipped six independent reliability/tooling/feature phases, specified in docs/v1.4/SPEC.md.
Tabs carry a workspace key through a workspaceKeyOf(tab) derivation on TabRecord, rather than through a second AppMode value.
Entries in the issues file are closed in place (status flips to Fixed, commit noted) rather than deleted.

### v1.5 C1 Tree-sitter parsing pipeline, cached in SQLite
Phase v1.5 C1 parses a repository's source files with tree-sitter and persists the trees and symbol tables in a SQLite cache.
The parse cache is keyed so that both a later phase's graph build and a live editor's incremental reparse read from it.
Grammars in scope: Java, Python, JavaScript, TypeScript (and TSX), Go, Rust, plus JSON and HTML, and CSS as the necessary companion of HTML, Vue and Svelte.
React (JSX/TSX) has no separate grammar: the JS/TS grammar covers it, plus AST-level component/hook conventions that the graph phase reads.
Vue .vue single-file components use their own block-based tree-sitter grammar; template, script and style blocks are each reparsed with their own grammar.
Svelte .svelte files have the same SFC shape as Vue.
Angular needs no grammar beyond TypeScript plus HTML: it is TypeScript decorators plus inline or external HTML templates.
File-save events already flow through internal/gitclient's repo watcher (fsevents on darwin, fsnotify elsewhere) for git-status purposes.
The C1 planning pass must decide whether the parse cache subscribes to the existing gitclient watcher or needs its own, and how a tree-sitter incremental byte-range edit maps onto the watcher's coarser change-set.
The C1 planning pass must choose the SQLite layout (new kira.db tables or a dedicated cache database) and the Go tree-sitter binding.
Tree-sitter grammars are C, so a cgo-free Go binding is not guaranteed for every listed language; the app prefers pure-Go drivers where one exists and a justified exception where not, and the plan must state what it found and what it costs.
C1 is the foundation for C2's graph, C3's MCP server and C5's Monaco definition provider, which all read parse output; fixing the cache/reparse contract first avoids each phase inventing its own.

### v1.5 C2 Code graph: go-to-definition, go-to-implementation, find references
C2 (v1.5) builds a navigable code graph over C1's parsed symbol tables, with per-file declarations and references resolved and linked across files within a repository.
C2 exposes a query surface of definitionOf(file, position), implementationsOf(...) and referencesTo(...), called by both the MCP server (C3) and the in-app editor (C6).
C2's scope matches C1's grammar list.
Framework-specific resolution rules (Vue SFC script setup bindings into template, React component/hook references, Angular decorators wiring a template to a class) are C2's own design surface.
C2's planning pass must state which framework constructs get real cross-block resolution and which stay at the plain-symbol level tree-sitter alone gives for a first pass, and say plainly where it draws that line.
Listing "support" for a framework in the user's ask does not obligate C2 to build every framework-specific inference it theoretically could.
The C2 code graph is persisted in the same SQLite cache C1 established and is invalidated incrementally on the same reparse signal.
C2 is its own phase because graph construction and resolution is the genuinely novel algorithmic piece of the chapter; this isolates it from C1's parsing mechanics and C5/C6's editor wiring so each phase's plan is reviewable alone.

### v1.5 C3 MCP repository-map server
The v1.5 C3 MCP repository-map server is a local MCP server exposing C2's code graph (definitions, references, implementations, symbol/file search) as tools an AI client calls instead of reading whole files, to reduce token usage.
The MCP repository-map server is disabled by default.
The settings surface has an enable toggle that, once on, shows the exact CLI command to register the server with the Claude Code CLI before an Install button next to it, so enabling is never a silent action.
Registering the MCP server as a VS Code extension's MCP server gets the same treatment: command shown first, then an install action.
The MCP server runs headless via a bun run script in this repo's own package.json, matching every other dev/build/test entry point.
Write the MCP server so running it during this repo's development never collides with the dev server, the git socket, or any port/file lock that wails3 task dev holds.
In the C3 planning pass, state the concrete isolation mechanism (a distinct port/socket, a lock file, or a design needing neither) rather than asserting non-interference without one.
The MCP server reads the same SQLite cache that C1/C2 populate, with no second parse pipeline.
C3 was moved up to follow C2 because it depends only on C2, never on Monaco, search, quick-open, or the native git graph (C5 onward), so its value (whether it cuts token usage on a real task) can be measured right after C2.

### v1.5 C4 CLAUDE.md: document the repository-map MCP server for this repo's own development
CLAUDE.md must include a section naming the C3 repository-map MCP server and explaining how a session working in this repo enables and configures it.
Enabling the repository-map MCP server is expected practice for development in this repo; CLAUDE.md says so to make the shipped capability discoverable to the next session.
The C4 CLAUDE.md documentation is written right after C3, because it documents C3's real shape rather than a planned one.
CLAUDE.md contains the rule "prune as you go, don't just append", which favors documenting once the thing documented is real.

### v1.5 C5 Native code-viewing workspace, shell and viewer: repo import, tabs, project tree, Monaco file viewer — read-only thro...
An imported git repository is stored as a parallel code_repos entry, listed in the same panel as database connections (ProjectPanel.vue's "Connections" list), not as an extension of ConnectionSummary.
Clicking an imported repo opens it as its own independent workspace with its own tab set, never interleaved with another open repo's tabs or with the shared strip of studio/api.
Per-repo tab isolation is a new workspaceId dimension on TabRecord, not a second AppMode value, so studio/api behave byte-identically to today.
In the code workspace, a single click opens a file into one shared, replaceable preview slot (VS Code-style preview tab); a double-click promotes it to a permanent tab that survives the next preview open.
The first tab of a repo workspace is permanently pinned, unclosable and un-reorderable, reserved for the git graph (C10); C5 builds the pinning mechanism and a placeholder view, and C10 replaces it with the real graph.
The repo project tree lists every tracked or untracked-but-not-ignored file in the repository and opens any of them into a tab; it is a new tree, not ProjectTree.vue, which is schema-object-specific.
monaco-editor (new dependency) is the file viewer, with its bundled language-service workers (editor, typescript, json, css, html) disabled, used strictly as a renderer with Monarch tokenizers for highlighting; definition/hover extension points are reserved for C6 to wire.
The Monaco viewer is always readOnly: true, and no tab in the code workspace ever gains dirty-state, save or commit machinery; the definition tab kind's no-badge shape is the precedent to mirror.
C6's diff tabs and navigation, C7's search results, C9's quick-open, C10's graph and C11's review layer all open into the C5 workspace, so per-repo tab isolation is resolved in C5 to avoid each later phase inventing its own.
C5 was split from a larger C5 because repo import plus tab isolation and Monaco integration were too much for one implementation pass; this half is usable on its own (import a repo, browse its tree, view a file, read-only).
A result click from search (C7) is wired into C5's workspace as a preview tab.

### v1.5 C6 Diff tabs and code navigation
v1.5 C6 covers Monaco's diff-editor as its own read-only tab (worktree-vs-HEAD from a file's changed status, opened from C5's project tree).
C6 was split out of the original single C5 phase during that phase's planning pass.
C6 includes the per-repository codeindex open/sync/watch lifecycle, reusing C1's per-repository sync lock so the desktop app and a headless bun run mcp:repo-map instance never parse one repository twice.
C6 wires Monaco's definition-provider and hover extension points to C2's codegraph package, including the byte-offset-to-UTF-16-column conversion that C1's plan assigned to this later phase.
A cross-file jump in C6 opens its target as a preview tab in C5's workspace.
C6 is kept as its own row, plan and verification pass, with its own commit history and test pass against a shell C5 has already proven correct, because its failure modes (worker/bundler/provider-registration) are unrelated to C5's shared tab-state rewrite.
C6 is sequenced right after C5.

### v1.5 C7 Search: in-file and repository-wide, Go-native
Phase v1.5 C7 provides two search surfaces: in-file search using Monaco's own find widget scoped to the open buffer, and a repository-wide search panel implemented in Go.
Never use a ripgrep subprocess for repository-wide search; it must be Go-native, per explicit correction.
C7's plan chooses between a hand-rolled walk-and-scan (reusing C1's or C9's file-enumeration source, bufio plus stdlib regexp/substring matching, run concurrently across files) and a well-maintained pure-Go search library; hand-rolled is chosen only if it clears CLAUDE.md's library-reuse bar on its own merits.
The C7 plan must state which search implementation was chosen and why, as C1 did for its tree-sitter binding choice.
Stream repository search matches as they arrive instead of buffering a whole run, because every operation exceeding ~150ms must show progress.
Clicking a search match opens the file into C5's workspace as a preview tab, following the convention C5 establishes.
C7 search is text-based, not symbol-based, so it is independent of C2's graph, but it depends on C5 existing as the place results open into.

### v1.5 C8 Repo-map MCP: source-line context on results
Phase C8 adds the literal source line at each hit's position to the rendered output of find_definition, find_references, find_implementations and search_symbols in the Repo-map MCP.
outline_file's per-node lines are unchanged by C8 (no source line per node), because it is capped at 500 nodes and a source line per node would multiply the size of a response whose value is compactness.
Since C3 the server never reads file bytes (render.go's comment says so); responses derive from codegraph's indexed metadata (positions, kinds, names). C8 crosses this boundary by reading source lines, read-only.
C8 requires that a source line read from a file changed since the last codeindex sync be labeled stale, never silently presented as current.
C8 requires a per-line length cap so one very long source line cannot blow up an otherwise compact response.
C8's planning pass decides how a line is read (one open-plus-seek per hit, or something cheaper).
C8 was motivated by C7's A/B-test dogfooding: Arm A's MCP-assisted work needed an outline_file/find_definition call plus a follow-up Read of the same file to see the signature text, a two-call pattern Aider's repo-map avoids by showing the definition line verbatim.
C7's A/B-test implementation stage should be re-run against the improved server after C8 before treating the first-round numbers as final, so C8 lands now rather than at the chapter's end.

### v1.5 C9 Quick Open: fuzzy file search (Cmd+P)
C9 (v1.5) is Quick Open, a fuzzy file search bound to Cmd+P, in the style of a command palette.
Quick Open searches the open repository's tracked and untracked-but-not-ignored files.
Quick Open opens the chosen file into C5's workspace as a preview tab, the same open convention C7's search results use.
The existing fuzzy-match precedent is frontend/src/shortcuts/CommandPalette.vue, which matches commands, not files; C9 should either reuse it or deliberately decline it.
C9's planning pass must state which existing file-listing source it reads from: C5's project tree listing or the repository listing C1's parse pipeline already enumerates. Reusing one avoids a second file-enumeration mechanism if its shape fits.
C9 depends on C1's file enumeration and C5's file-opening. It is sequenced last among the editor-surface phases because it is the thinnest of the three and benefits most from the others being in place.

### v1.5 C10 Git graph mounted natively, pinned per repo
Phase C10 mounts packages/git-ui's existing graph natively, reusing the exact same Vue components as the VS Code extension (CommitGrid.vue, graph/rowSvg/layout.worker geometry engine, branch/tag/stash/worktree/stack panels, search, undo) with no reimplementation.
The native git graph mounts into the permanently pinned first tab that C5 reserved in every repository workspace, replacing C5's placeholder, except the code-review layer, which is deferred to C11.
File opens from the graph route into C5's Monaco viewer as new tabs; diff opens route through C6's diff-tab mechanism as new tabs, instead of VS Code's native editor.
Keep the native git graph read-only: disable or hide git-ui's stage/unstage, commit, discard and undo write actions, because reusing a component does not mean reusing its write affordances.
git-ui's bridge/client.ts currently assumes the extension's transport shape: a paired client token over git.sock, per git_clients' trust-store design (G1).
The native app's queries to its own backend need the same internal/gitrpc handler table but not the paired-client authentication that gates an external VS Code process; C10's plan must decide between a second in-process transport and the existing socket reused with a self-trusted client.
C10 is sequenced after C5 (tab/workspace shell and pinned-tab mechanism) and C6 (diff-tab mechanism); C2/C9 are optional inputs, so C10 can land once C5+C6 are ready even if C2/C7/C9 slip.

### v1.5 C11 Code-review layer ported natively (last feature phase)
v1.5 C11 ports packages/git-ui components/review/ and state/review*.ts into the native workspace established by C10, reusing the exact same Vue components the extension uses.
The code-review layer covers inline comment threads over a diff, AI-review feedback shown via gutter icons on affected lines, and a PR-review workflow.
The review layer depends on the graph, C5's Monaco viewer, C6's diff view (renders what a comment thread anchors to) and C2's navigation already being in place and proven.
Where the review layer's panel lives in the native workspace (own tab, side panel beside the diff, or other) is an open design question; the plan must state what it tried and why it landed where it did.
Gutter-icon anchoring uses Monaco's own decoration/glyph-margin extension points against a diff view's line mapping; no comment-anchoring-over-Monaco precedent exists in this repo, so it is this phase's own design surface.
In the extension, apps/kira-studio-vscode/src/reviewView.ts, reviewComments.ts, reviewRanges.ts and reviewMarking.ts host the review surface; the C11 plan must state what changes versus that.
The review surface in the native workspace is read-only: it views and reacts to review feedback and never writes code.
C11 is the last among feature phases, because the mounted graph, native diff viewer, navigation and gutter/decoration wiring are safer to build against once proven than co-developed.

### v1.5 C12 Code review, round 1
Code review runs once chapters C1-C11 are complete, using three Opus subagents in parallel, one per dimension, each reporting findings only and never fixing.
The three code review dimensions are: architecture/structure/maintainability/security; functional correctness/business logic; performance/resource efficiency.
Code review scope is the full diff the chapter introduces (branch base to tip) as the primary target; a reviewer that notices a real issue outside the diff, in a file the chapter touches or adjacent to one, reports and fixes it too.
After review, one sequential Sonnet subagent fixes every finding, in parallel only for a batch genuinely isolated from another, and commits each fix individually.
No findings document survives a code review round once fixed; the commit log is the record.
Run the code review round once the chapter's feature work is otherwise done, because it is a standing instruction, not a one-off request, even though CLAUDE.md's trigger is "on request".

### v1.5 C13 Code review, round 2
On repeated review rounds, if a dimension finds nothing real, state so plainly instead of manufacturing a finding (CLAUDE.md rule).
CLAUDE.md says multiple review passes means repeating the whole loop.
Code review round 2 (C13) repeats the three-dimension review plus fix cycle as a fresh reading of the tree as C12's fixes left it, not a rerun against C12's summary.

### v1.5 C14 Code review, round 3 (fable reviewers)
In v1.5 C14 (code review round 3), the three parallel reviewing subagents run on the fable model instead of Opus, per explicit instruction. Structure and scope match C12/C13 (same three dimensions).
C14 is a differently-modeled reviewer pass over a tree that two Opus rounds already reviewed, because a different reviewer model is expected to surface different findings.
The fix step in C14 stays a Sonnet subagent, unchanged from C12/C13.
Review rounds are ordered with two same-model rounds first, to converge on what one reviewer model consistently catches, then one differently-modeled round last, to check what that model misses.
<!-- /chapter v1.5 -->

<!-- chapter v1.6 -->
## v1.6
Source: docs/v1.6/ at bd0c224688d9. 23 phases.
The v1.6 chapter has six independent phases across existing modules: editor consolidation (CodeMirror removed, Monaco everywhere), a dependency/runtime upgrade, a native git-blame widget, an S3/Redis browse-panel rework, an unsigned-app update-availability banner, and foreign-key-preview edit mode.
The v1.6 chapter continues P numbering (P60+) rather than taking a fresh letter, as v1.1, v1.2 and v1.4 did, because its phases are unrelated rather than one subsystem.
v1.5 shipped code intelligence as one cohesive chapter, specified in docs/v1.5/SPEC.md (C1-C14).
Entries are closed in place (status flips to Fixed, commit noted) rather than deleted.
Code-intelligence tools reject a wrong argument name with a schema-validation error; for example outline_file rejects path as an unexpected additional property, and the real parameter is file.
Read the server port from startup output rather than assuming the documented 8765, because the server may pick another port.
Code-intelligence lookups wait out an in-flight rescan instead of answering from a mid-rebuild index, and report that the index is not ready when the initial sync has not completed, rather than returning an empty result or "no symbol found".
On cross-language name collisions, find_definition and related lookups report every hit rather than the first, and rank the referring file's own language first.
The repo derives most of its shared domain types across languages, so cross-language name collisions have real impact; outline_file on packages/shared/domain/tree.ts returns 23 nodes after the fix rather than 7.

### v1.6 P60 Editor consolidation: remove CodeMirror, Monaco everywhere
Phase P60 (v1.6) removes all @codemirror/* packages (autocomplete, commands, lang-json, lang-sql, lang-xml, language, lint, merge, state, view) and replaces every surface that uses them with Monaco.
Monaco was already brought into the app in v1.5 for the native code workspace: lazy-load pattern in apps/kira-studio/frontend/src/views/repo/monaco.ts, language registration in language.ts.
CodeMirror is used via shared editor/CodeMirrorHost.vue (supported by editor/{theme,languages,wrapSelection,findRanges,hover,diagnostics,variableHighlight}.ts), mounted by the SQL query console (views/console/*) and by project/SchemaDialog.vue's DDL editor.
The console files sqlLanguageService.ts, completion.ts, sqlHover.ts, sqlDiagnostics.ts, ddl.ts and lezerNodes.ts reference Lezer/CodeMirror-specific APIs.
A diff/merge view probably exists elsewhere on CodeMirror (since @codemirror/merge is a dependency), even though Monaco already owns diff rendering in the repo workspace (v1.5 C6); P60's planning pass must find every such site.
Each CodeMirror site must have its capabilities replaced in Monaco: SQL-aware autocomplete over live schema metadata (state/schemaColumns.ts), lint/diagnostics, DDL-document completion, hover, and whatever the merge view provides.
P60 does not ship until every listed capability has a working Monaco equivalent verified feature-by-feature, not merely that the editor renders.
P60 is scheduled first in the chapter because it is the largest, most cross-cutting change, and later phases (e.g. P67, whose cell editor may be CodeMirror-based) should build on a final single-engine editing surface.

### v1.6 P61 Dependency and runtime upgrade
Phase v1.6 P61 upgrades the toolchain and every major dependency to their current latest stable versions.
The root go.mod uses Go 1.27.0, and Wails is pinned to the pre-release github.com/wailsapp/wails/v3 v3.0.0-beta.16, a beta dependency the whole app builds on.
The Wails dependency needs explicit attention rather than a routine bump, because a Wails v3 stable release or a beta-to-beta jump can carry breaking API changes to internal/shell's own usage.
P61's planning pass builds the full current-vs-latest version inventory (Go direct dependencies, all frontend packages in the Bun workspace, Bun/Node runtime) in the plan doc, not the phase row, because a version table goes stale between planning and implementation.
The frontend packages in P61's scope include vue, the Vite/rolldown build tooling, monaco-editor, slickgrid, flatbuffers, zod, tree-sitter grammars, @vscode/codicons and fuzzysort.
Investigate any deliberate historical version pin in code comments or docs/DEV_ENVIRONMENT.md (a compatibility workaround, not mere staleness), and either resolve it or re-affirm it with its reason restated; never silently bump past it.
P61 is sequenced immediately after P60, which already changes package.json by removing a whole dependency family, so the broader bump lands on that changed tree as one deliberate pass instead of a second large independent dependency churn.

### v1.6 P62 Git blame widget in the native git module
v1.6 P62 ports git blame into the natively-embedded git module (the read-only packages/git-ui mount from v1.5 C10/C11) and is not new backend work.
v1.4 P5 already built the blame backend for the VS Code extension's status bar: internal/gitrpc's blame.line method, a porcelain-parser-backed git blame run through internal/gitclient's streaming runner, and the packages/git-ipc contract method.
blame.line is already in the read-only allowlist in v1.5's internal/bridge/gitstream.go, admitted as a pure read, so it is safe to call from the native surface.
P62's work is a native-side UI consumer of blame.line that follows C11's porting discipline: reuse packages/git-ui components/state where they exist and build only what the native embedding lacks.
P62's planning pass decides where blame surfaces in the native workspace: inline gutter annotations over Monaco (matching C11's comment-decoration precedent), a status-bar-style summary (the extension's original shape), or both.
P62 is independent of every other row in its chapter.

### v1.6 P63 Browse panels: vertical split, relocated back-navigation, clearer Redis types
P63 (browse panels: vertical split, relocated back-navigation, clearer Redis types) is planned in docs/v1.6/plans/P63-browse-split-and-redis-types.md; its implementation is deferred and lands as part of P65, behind P64 and P65 in landing order.
There is no separate S3/Redis browse panel pair: one BrowseView.vue serves both engines, joined with the separate KeyValueView.vue data-view tab via an extracted KeyValuePane.vue.
PanelSplitter, not PanelShell, is the vehicle for the P63 split.
The browse back control is already an icon (arrow-up) and becomes chevron-left, not arrow-left, because arrow-left is already this app's Previous-page glyph.
Redis type differentiation in the browse tree uses icon plus badge, not colour, modeled on RedisInsight.
Redis type differentiation requires a small backend addition, Adapter.KeyTypes, because the tree carries no type today.

### v1.6 P64 Repo-map MCP: fix the type-alias gap, investigate what else the graph can answer
In the repo-map MCP server (internal/repomap), TypeScript type aliases (every z.infer-derived domain type such as TreeNode, BrowseTabState, and every *TabRecord) are invisible to the index.
When a TypeScript type alias is missing from the index, find_definition does not error; it silently returns a same-named Go struct instead, which is a correctness bug.
CLAUDE.md's dogfooding rule requires the type-alias gap in the repo-map MCP to be closed before the next phase starts, so the fix is a hard prerequisite regardless of P65.
The repo-map MCP server has six tools: find_definition, find_references, find_implementations, search_symbols, search_files, outline_file.
A signature/pattern search over already-extracted declarations is a confirmed candidate new repo-map tool, motivated by P63's planning hunting for a function doing bulk writes with no name to search by.
A new repo-map capability earns a place only if it demonstrably reduces the tokens an implementing agent spends getting an answer it would otherwise reach a slower or larger-context way; it must not be added for completeness or IDE-feature symmetry.
For each proposed repo-map capability, the planning pass must state what token cost it removes and how that claim is checked.
The type-alias fix lands between P63's plan and its implementation so that P65's A/B measurement measures the tool in the state a real user would have it, not a known-incomplete one.

### v1.6 P64b Repo-map MCP: close the same alias/constant gap in Go and plain JavaScript
P64's fix (typescript/p64_declarations.scm and tsx/p64_declarations.scm) closed the missing-capture defect for TypeScript only; the same defect class exists in Go and plain JavaScript.
In go/tags.scm, the const_declaration/var_declaration patterns capture a bare @name with no @definition.x wrapper, so top-level Go const/var produce no symbol row at all.
extract.go's code comment states that a capture with neither a definition. nor reference. prefix produces nothing.
Go type coverage is healthy, with a 1274-symbol baseline that P64's plan used for comparison.
javascript/tags.scm captures arrow/function-valued const/let/var as @definition.function by design, but a non-function top-level const/let such as export const CONFIG is captured only via a narrow CommonJS-shaped export_statement value assignment_expression pattern, not ESM's export const form.
HTML, CSS, JSON and Svelte are correctly symbol-free by design, because a CSS selector or a JSON key is not a definition anything resolves to (per the symbolLanguages comment in languages.go).
Rust already captures its type-like declarations, bucketed under @definition.class; this is a labeling quirk, not a missing capture.
Python and Java are out of scope for P64b; Java's missing-enum gap is logged separately and deferred.
P64b's planning pass confirms exact scope against the live query files and codeindex.db as P64's did, and measures how much of this repository's own .js/.go source is affected before committing to the fix's shape.
P64b gets its own row rather than reopening P64's approved plan, and is sequenced before P65 so the A/B measurement reflects the tool's fully current state.

### v1.6 P64c Repo-map MCP: make a full parse-from-scratch fast
P64c target: a full parse of a repository of about 1800 files, from nothing, takes seconds, regardless of what triggers it.
The repo-map MCP server's documented baseline for a full index of about 1800 files is about 30s.
P64c scope is the raw throughput of the parse-and-write pipeline; how often a full reparse is triggered (e.g. a query-file edit forcing truncate-and-reparse) is out of scope, as a dev-only, low-frequency cost.
P64c must investigate whether file parsing can be parallelized across goroutines without breaking codeindex's per-repo write ordering.
P64c must investigate whether SQLite writes are batched into transactions or issued one at a time, and whether the watcher/sync-writer lock contention is structural or a one-off from running several repo-map instances in one container.
The slow full index observed during P64b was one genuinely single-threaded parse-and-write pass (no deadlock, no infinite loop), with SQLITE_BUSY "database is locked" errors from the fsnotify watcher and the sync writer contending for the same SQLite file.
More goroutines is only one plausible lever for P64c, not the presumed answer; the planning pass must profile the actual pipeline (time and lock contention) before proposing a fix, per CLAUDE.md's "measure when there's a real, concrete question at stake".
A first-ever index of a brand-new repository pays the same full-parse cost, so P64c speeds up a real user's first run, not just dev convenience.
P64c is sequenced before P65 so the A/B measurement's "with MCP" arm reflects a server that is fast to (re)index.

### v1.6 P65 A/B measurement: implement P63 with and without the repo-map MCP server
Phase P65 implements P63's approved plan twice from the identical starting commit, in two isolated worktrees: one Sonnet subagent using the repo-map MCP server (with P64's improvements) as CLAUDE.md instructs, and one using Grep/Read only.
In P65 both arms implement to real shipping quality so the comparison is fair.
The MCP-using arm's implementation is the real one and lands as P63's actual deliverable on v1.6; the non-MCP arm is a throwaway baseline discarded after comparison.
P65's deliverable beyond the shipped feature is the measurement: total tokens and tool-call counts for each arm, reported plainly, replacing the repo-map MCP section's asserted-but-never-measured savings claim with a real number.
P65 requires P63's plan and P64's improved, corrected tool to have landed first, so the with-MCP arm reflects the tool's real current capability.

### v1.6 P66 Update-availability banner (no silent auto-update)
The app is distributed unsigned, so a real auto-download-and-install update mechanism is not viable; P66 uses an update-availability banner with no silent auto-update.
P66 fetches the latest GitHub release for this repository and compares it against the running app's version from internal/buildinfo, which already holds build/version metadata.
Only when a newer release exists, a status bar banner appears; clicking it opens the GitHub releases page in the user's OS-default browser.
The status bar is an existing UI region, referenced in TitleBar.vue, WorkbenchShell.vue and SettingsDialog.vue.
Public release metadata needs no per-user credential, so an anonymous list-releases call suffices; internal/ghclient is already used by the git module's commit.resolvePr/branch.resolvePr.
P66 is independent of every other row.

### v1.6 P67 Cell editor: enter edit mode for a foreign-key preview's related record
P67 (v1.6) extends the FK preview in the cell editor so it opens the related record in a real edit session, not only a read-only preview.
Clicking a foreign-key-valued cell already shows the referenced row from its own table; the component is likely views/shared/celleditor/CellEditorView.vue or adjacent.
P67 design: route the FK preview into the existing row-edit surface, pointed at the related table/row, reusing it rather than building a second edit UI.
P67 is sequenced after P60, so the cell editor is already Monaco-based; P67 builds on that end state. P67 is independent of every other feature row.

### v1.6 P67b Git module: peer top-level module, graph/loading fixes, file-tree icon parity
Phase P67b makes Git a peer top-level module alongside Studio and Api, each self-contained, with repositories switched inside the Git module.
A repo does not displace the studio/api switcher; it already sits beside it as a fourth-plus top-level tab.
The real complaint behind P67b: a repository (an instance) is a top-level peer of modules, and the repo list lives inside Studio's Connections panel.
P67b fixes a bug where the graph and review tab went blank after opening a file, because a shared transport's owner disposed a socket other mounts still held a reference to.
P67b fixes a bug where a cold start needed a manual Retry, because a stream send raced the socket's own open event.
P67b ports file-tree icons verbatim from the diff tree's own icon rule, for icon parity.
The read-only-relaxation piece of the original finding was split out of P67b into P67e.
The P67b plan lives at docs/v1.6/plans/P67b-git-module-peer-nav.md, and the phase is independent of every other row.

### v1.6 P67e Git module: relax read-only for pull/fetch/merge/rebase/push, add the native credential prompt
P67e admits every mutating git operation that writes through git itself rather than an in-app file edit, including all op.run/remote.run kinds (fetch, pull, push, force-push, undo, restack); before it, the native git module is read-only.
P67e keeps refused the operations that need something the app lacks: conflict resolution, a worktree prepare script's arbitrary shell execution, opening a worktree window, and setting the global git path.
A conflicting merge/rebase must surface the conflict state plainly through the existing conflict banner, widened so Continue/Skip/Abort work, rather than attempt a resolution the app can't offer.
P67e builds a native credential prompt for HTTPS auth, because nothing native answers credential.request today.
P67e's planning pass re-verifies P67b's §5 research (docs/v1.6/plans/P67b-git-module-peer-nav.md) against the tree P67b lands, since line numbers and some files move.
P67e needs P67b landed first because it shares hostHandlers.ts's capability-flag literal and is easier to verify on the finished nav reorg tree.

### v1.6 P67f Repo-map MCP: distinguish "not yet indexed" from "genuinely absent"; fix `find_references` missing range/index-expres...
Against a cold index, repo-map MCP search_symbols answered "no symbols matching" for an existing symbol (readOnlyMethods, internal/bridge/gitstream.go:70), then answered correctly about 90 seconds later with no restart.
internal/repomap/render.go:140 renders the identical string for "the index has not caught up yet" and "this symbol does not exist", and nothing in internal/repomap exposes sync state an MCP client could check first.
Repo-map MCP find_references returns nothing, even on a warm index, for a package-level variable whose only reads are via range or an index expression (reproduced with allowedMethods, allowedStreamMethods, writeMethods, hostAnsweredMethods).
The find_references range/index-expression gap is logged in docs/v1.6/mcp-repo-map-issues.md and is likely a tags.scm pattern gap, the same defect class as P64/P64b but on the reference side rather than the definition side.
The server already tracks sync-in-progress state internally (a watcher/writer state used for its own logging, from P64c's throughput work); P67f plans to surface it either as a distinct "still indexing" response shape or a tools/list-visible sync-status tool, whichever costs an implementing agent fewer tokens.
Fix non-trivial dogfooding findings in a dedicated pass before the next phase starts; do not let them ride along unfixed (as P63's finding forced P64), per CLAUDE.md.

### v1.6 P67c Monaco theming/highlighting fixes, app-wide checkbox styling, markdown reading view
Phase v1.6 P67c bundles Monaco theming/highlighting fixes, app-wide checkbox styling, and a markdown reading view into one phase, at the user's request.
Monaco syntax highlighting is inconsistent: package.json and TypeScript decorators are not highlighted properly; the planning pass must find every language-registration gap behind these (likely views/repo/language.ts or monacoEntry.ts), not just the two reported cases.
Monaco's right-click context menu and autocomplete widget render unthemed across most Monaco panels (transparent background, black text); the fix is to use Monaco's editor.defineTheme API, already used elsewhere in the app, rather than default browser-native popup styling.
Checkbox styling is broken app-wide; the fix is to locate the shared checkbox primitive/CSS and fix it in that one place.
For markdown files, a button toggles a rendered, reading-formatted view (rendered Markdown, not a code formatter) alongside the existing raw/code view.
Phase P67c is independent of every other row in the plan.

### v1.6 P67d Repo-map MCP settings toggle: general enablement, per-repo access
The Settings dialog's Code intelligence tab is the product surface for the repo-map MCP toggle, distinct from the dev-only headless server that CLAUDE.md's own section covers.
The repo-map MCP toggle fails with "no repository found for this working directory" because it is scoped to a single working directory, not app-wide.
P67d makes the repo-map MCP toggle general: enabling it starts the server with no working-directory requirement, lists every repo the app has imported/opened, and lets the user grant or revoke MCP access per repo.

### v1.6 P68 Code review, round 1
P68 code review round 1 uses three parallel Opus subagents, one per dimension: architecture/security, functional correctness, performance/resource efficiency. They produce findings only, per CLAUDE.md's own process.
After the P68 review, one sequential Sonnet subagent fixes every finding judged real.
P68 code review runs only after every feature row (P60-P67, P67b-P67f) has landed, because a review needs a finished tree.
A final row (P70) updates this repository's main docs.

### v1.6 P68b Repo-map MCP: fix `outline_file`'s absent-file vs. no-definitions ambiguity
In the repo-map MCP, outline_file renders the identical sentence "%s has no indexed definitions" (render.go:181) for both an indexed file with no definitions and a nonexistent path, so an agent cannot tell a typo'd path from a definition-free file.
In the repo-map MCP, search_files already answers whether a path exists, and sourceForOneFile already distinguishes file not found from unreadable for its source-line reads.
Phase P68b wires the file-not-found vs. unreadable distinction (as in sourceForOneFile) into outline_file to resolve the absent-file vs. no-definitions ambiguity.
The outline_file ambiguity was found during P68's dimension-1 review by calling the tool rather than reading source, and is logged in docs/v1.6/mcp-repo-map-issues.md under Non-trivial per CLAUDE.md's rule.
Close a non-trivial dogfooding finding before the next phase starts, because CLAUDE.md requires it; the outline_file finding therefore gates P69, not P68's fix pass, which covers only ordinary review findings.

### v1.6 P69 Code review, round 2
Code review round 2 (P69) reruns the same three-dimension review cycle in full on the tree that P68 and P68b leave; it is not optional, per CLAUDE.md's rule to repeat the whole loop that many times.
When a code review round finds nothing real, say so instead of manufacturing a finding.

### v1.6 P69b Repo-map MCP: capture a package-level constant/variable read as a plain argument/operand identifier
Repo-map MCP find_references returns "no references found" for a package-level constant or variable read as a plain identifier in argument or comparison/arithmetic-operand position, the commonest way such a name is read.
P67f's fix (ddd15b00/0bfef238) covered only range-clause and index-expression reads, so P69b is a narrower survivor of the same defect class, reproduced on six real names across Go and TypeScript.
P69b is logged in docs/v1.6/mcp-repo-map-issues.md under Non-trivial.
P69b's planning pass must measure the row-count growth of the new bare-identifier capture before shipping, because locals and parameters will dominate it and insertRef is already most of the write phase (per P64c's measurement), so index size and throughput are a concern.
CLAUDE.md requires a non-trivial dogfooding finding to be closed before the next phase starts, so P69b gates P70 but not P69's own fix pass, as P68b gated P69 rather than P68.

### v1.6 P69c `codeparse`: cancelling a parse mid-flight still crashes the process (C assertion, not the earlier SIGSEGV)
Cancelling a parse mid-flight in codeparse still crashes the process: TestParseConcurrentCancellationDoesNotCrash aborts the test binary on go-tree-sitter's C assertion 'ts_parser_parse: Assertion self->finished_tree.ptr failed' (SIGABRT), replacing the earlier SIGSEGV.
The SIGABRT stack is rooted in parseWithOptions in session.go, the function P69's commit b412286b rewrote to fix the earlier SIGSEGV; the crash mode changed but the underlying hazard (cancelling a context mid-parse) did not.
Session.Parse is the live indexing path, not test-only, so the crash reaches the shipped app: a Settings revoke or app quit racing a running index can trigger it.
P69c must find the safe cancellation boundary the go-tree-sitter C API supports; whether ParseWithOptions's progress-callback approach is unsafe to race against a concurrent cancel must be established against the library's C source, not assumed from its Go wrapper.
The P69c fix must be confirmed with the same repeated-run stress-test discipline P69's fix used to confirm the SIGSEGV was gone.
P69c is a crash-severity finding that gates P70 alongside P69b, and is sequenced after P69b lands (one phase at a time).

### v1.6 P69d Repo-map MCP: `find_references`'s `file` scoping is broken; investigate a workaround for `go-tree-sitter`'s per-parse...
In the repo-map MCP, find_references's file/line disambiguator, which the tool's own ambiguity message recommends, turns a correct answer into an empty one for at least one reproduced symbol.
In the repo-map MCP, find_references fails to filter by file/line for Parse: pinning file/line to its own definition still returns 38 unrelated hits.
In the repo-map MCP, the find_references languages filter widens its own candidate set rather than narrowing it, and accepts its values case-sensitively with no error on a bad case.
go-tree-sitter@v0.25.0's ParseWithOptions (parser.go:350, three more call sites, and query.go:788) saves its options struct into the global go-pointer registry with no matching Unref in the vendored source.
Every go-tree-sitter parse leaks one C.malloc(1) plus a permanently retained registry map entry, under a global mutex that all concurrent parses contend on.
No newer go-tree-sitter version exists to fix the pointer-registry leak upstream.
P69d investigates whether reusing one static ParseOptions/callback shape across calls can bound registry growth to a constant instead of one entry per parse, without reopening P69c's cancel-safety property.
P69d root-causes the find_references scoping bug directly against internal/repomap's resolution/filtering code, as prior repo-map fix rows in this chapter did.
Both P69c dogfooding findings are logged in docs/v1.6/mcp-repo-map-issues.md. CLAUDE.md's rule requires both closed before the next phase starts, so P69d gates P70 and is sequenced after P69c lands.

### v1.6 P69e `internal/repomap`: fix `TestDetachDrainsInFlightCall`'s pre-existing `-race` failure
TestDetachDrainsInFlightCall in internal/repomap fails under go test -race: Server.Detach spawns a goroutine that waits for in-flight callers to drain then calls close(), which reads package-level readyTimeout, while the test's deferred restore of readyTimeout can run before that goroutine finishes.
The fix for the TestDetachDrainsInFlightCall race is test-only: the test must wait for Detach's drain goroutine (via a hook or a bounded wait on an observable side effect) before its defer restores readyTimeout; no product code changes are expected.
A known-red go test -race result must not stand at chapter close, under the "no shortcuts, no TODO: fix later" bar, even when found by running the test suite rather than by a dogfooding tool call.
P69e gates P70, as every other lettered repo-map/test-suite finding in the v1.6 chapter has gated the next phase.

### v1.6 P70 Update main docs
Phase P70 updates the main docs (README.md, docs/ARCHITECTURE.md, docs/DEV_ENVIRONMENT.md, CLAUDE.md) to be current for everything v1.5 and v1.6 changed, including v1.5's whole code-intelligence subsystem (C1-C14) and every row P60-P69e.
P70 reads each main doc against the current tree, not the SPEC.md or plans/ prose of either chapter, to find drift such as a removed dependency still documented, a new subsystem missing from ARCHITECTURE.md's component list, a stale tool count, or a resolved environment quirk.
P70 is the last row: it needs every other row landed (including both code-review rounds, P68b, P69b, P69c, P69d and P69e), and it is the one row scoped to both v1.5 and v1.6.
<!-- /chapter v1.6 -->

## v1.7
Source: docs/v1.7/ at bd0c224688d9. 10 phases.
The v1.7 chapter builds a local database MCP server with list/metadata/query tools, a per-connection permissions model, and PII anonymization filters usable over MCP and in the normal data viewer.
v1.7 phases use the letter M (MCP) instead of continuing v1.6's P numbering, because every phase builds one subsystem, as v1.5's code-intelligence chapter did with C1-C14.
M1's migration is named 0020_m1_connection_mcp.sql after rebase.
M1c root cause was two-part: Go/TS/JS field declarations were never indexed as symbols, and a plain x.Field/obj.prop read was never captured as a reference (only call-position selectors were).
The M1c fix uses a new @reference.field kind rather than reusing @reference.read, because the cross-directory tier-clamp of @reference.read would have left the original bug unfixed.
Field indexing covers Go/TS/JS but is declined for Java/Python/Rust (2 fixture files each, no real user) and for object/composite-literal keys, which are syntactically ambiguous with map literals in Go (25,490:5,493 noise ratio in TS).
extractionVersion was bumped from 3 to 4, which forces a one-time full reindex on next sync; this is expected, not a bug.
After the M1c fix, search_symbols and find_references on ConnectionFields.AutoExplain resolve correctly, with exactly 7 references.
M4 (Faker-MCP bridge) never shipped.
Closed entries are updated in place (status flips to Fixed, commit noted) rather than deleted.

### v1.7 M1 Local DB MCP server: list/metadata/query tools, 7-day rotating token
The v1.7 M1 plan (docs/v1.7/plans/M1-db-mcp-server-core.md) adds a new Go MCP server for local databases, implemented as part of M1ab.
The DB MCP server is a sibling to internal/repomap and reuses its transport pattern (go-sdk/mcp, loopback-only streamable HTTP), but has its own process, port and tool set.
The DB MCP server exposes list_connections, list_databases, list_schemas, describe_table and run_query; list_connections returns every configured connection the app knows.
Metadata tools (list_databases, list_schemas, describe_table) route through the existing apps/kira-studio/internal/adapters/* layer, with no new metadata path.
run_query executes through the same adapter query path the console UI uses.
MCP auth is a static bearer token with a 7-day expiry that rotates, built directly into internal/mcpauth (not a sibling package).
The repo-map server's currently non-expiring token gets the same 7-day rotation in internal/mcpauth, by explicit instruction, rather than a second scheme.
The M1 plan designs the expiry/rotation record shape, the rotation trigger (auto-remint on expiry vs. manual remint, possibly differing for the headless repo-map server), the lapsed-token client error, the server start/stop lifecycle, and whether to share KIRA_HOME with the repo-map server or use a distinct root.
A client presenting a lapsed token must see a distinct, actionable error, not a generic 401.
M1 is the first phase: every later phase (permissions, EXPLAIN, Faker, anonymization) extends this server's tool set or query path, so nothing else in the chapter can start before it exists.
M1's migration is renumbered 0020_m1_connection_mcp.sql after the rebase.

### v1.7 M1ab A/B measurement: implement M1 with and without the repo-map MCP server
The v1.7 M1ab phase implemented M1's approved plan twice from the identical commit b46eac88 in two isolated worktrees: arm A used the repo-map MCP server for navigation, arm B used only Grep/Read.
Arm A (MCP) is M1's real deliverable on v1.7; arm B was a throwaway baseline discarded after comparison.
M1 was chosen as the A/B candidate because it is almost entirely Go with no .vue surface, so repo-map's confirmed Vue blind spot (docs/v1.6/mcp-repo-map-issues.md) does not undercut it, and it needs broad unfamiliar-codebase navigation.
In the M1 A/B measurement, the repo-map MCP arm used more tokens (516,104 vs 451,263) and more tool calls (339 vs 332) than the Grep/Read arm, so there was no overall efficiency win on this backend-heavy phase.
Repo-map saved specific reads: outline_file on page/builder.go avoided about 10 unneeded read_symbol calls, and read_symbol sized dbmcp's consumer interfaces without reading tree.Service's 300 lines.
Report A/B token and tool-call numbers plainly whether or not they favor the tool, as v1.6 P65 did.
Arm A's run_query forced a full reconnect (live adapter torn down, cache dropped, pre-connect script re-run) on every call even for an already-connected connection, and did not surface a failed connect's own error text.
Arm A's Playwright fixtures preconnect.spec.ts and secrets.spec.ts missed the new mcpEnabled field; a follow-up sweep found further tests/ui/ specs missing it too.
Arm B never hydrated settingsState.dbMcp from persisted settings, so the Database MCP panel, including Regenerate, appears disabled and hidden after every app restart even when the server is running.
Arm A's defects were fixed on v1.7-m1ab-arm-a: reconnect churn and error surfacing in 8e2542d9 with a regression test in access_test.go, and the mcpEnabled fixture sweep across all tests/ui/ specs in 764f6e61.
The M1 work landed on v1.7 via non-fast-forward merge commit dfa45459, because v1.7 gained two doc-only commits after the worktrees branched.
The full tests/ui/ Playwright suite must run with a KIRA_DEBUG_HOOKS=1 test build; post-merge it passed 269/269 on real webkit.
Verify claims of pre-existing failures against the actual built base commit, not an arm's self-report, because a fixture failure claimed pre-existing was a real regression.

### v1.7 M1c Repo-map MCP: fix the struct-field reference/definition gap
In the repo-map MCP, find_references/search_symbols returned nothing for a Go struct field reached only via a selector expression (e.g. f.AutoExplain).
The struct-field gap had two root causes: Go/TS/JS field declarations were never indexed as symbols, and a plain x.Field/obj.prop read was never captured as a reference (only call-position selectors were).
The fix uses a new @reference.field capture kind instead of reusing @reference.read, because @reference.read's cross-directory tier-clamp would have left the original bug unfixed.
The field fix covers Go and TypeScript/JS (same defect class, P64b precedent); it is declined for Java/Python/Rust because each has only 2 fixture files and no real user.
Object/composite-literal keys are not captured as field references, because they are syntactically ambiguous with map literals in Go and had a 25,490:5,493 noise ratio in TS.
extractionVersion was bumped from 3 to 4, which forces a one-time full reindex on next sync; this is expected, not a bug.
After the fix, search_symbols/find_references on ConnectionFields.AutoExplain resolve correctly, giving exactly 7 references at exact lines.
Close the struct-field reference gap before the next phase's implementation starts, as CLAUDE.md requires (same gating as v1.6's P64/P68b/P69d/P69e fixes).

### v1.7 M2 Connection editor: MCP tab, per-operation permissions (read/write/DDL), deny/allow/prompt enforcement
v1.7 M2 adds an MCP tab to the existing tab strip in ConnectionDialog.vue; DetailTab gains a fourth value.
The MCP tab has a free-text description field ("what this DB is for"), passed to the AI client as connection metadata, likely surfaced via the response of list_connections/describe_table.
The MCP tab has three independent permission controls (read, write, DDL), each set to deny, allow or prompt.
Prompt mode means the query does not run until a human approves it, shown with the actual query text, in an approval UI (dialog or persistent queue/dock) that the phase's planning pass designs, building on existing interrupt UI such as the conflict banner precedent in v1.6's P67e row.
M2's backend extends ConnectionFields with the description and three permission-mode columns via a new migration, following the precedent of 0004_p18_auto_explain.sql.
Permission enforcement happens in M1's run_query tool handler, which classifies each statement as read, write or DDL before executing it. The planning pass decides how to classify reliably per adapter kind (SQL parse or per-kind heuristic); Mongo, Redis and S3 have no single statement shape, so it must generalize past SQL.
M2 follows M1c's gating dogfooding finding and extends M1's single query-execution tool and the connection model directly.

### v1.7 M3 EXPLAIN path over MCP: parsed responses, auto-force-explain, heavy-query flag
The MCP server exposes an explain_query tool that returns the same parsed plan structure the console UI already renders (via parseExplainPages in views/console/explain.ts and plan.ts), not a raw driver dump.
An auto-force-explain setting, per-connection or per-request, runs EXPLAIN before every run_query call on the MCP path regardless of whether the client asks for it.
When the parsed plan crosses a heaviness threshold, the query is flagged back to the human user, not silently to the AI.
The heaviness threshold is defined in this phase's planning pass, based on signals such as row-estimate, seq-scan-on-large-table and cost value that plan.ts's existing parse already exposes cheaply, extended only if genuinely needed.
M3 is scheduled right after M2 because both extend M1's query tool, and the permission gate for heavy/DDL queries should exist before EXPLAIN-gating logic is layered on top.

### v1.7 M4 Faker exposed via MCP — descoped, not built
v1.7 M4 (exposing Faker via MCP) was descoped and never built; nothing from M4 ships.
M4 was designed as a generate_fake_data tool bridging the Go MCP server to @faker-js/faker through an embedded goja JS VM; design kept as a historical record in docs/v1.7/plans/M4-faker-bridge.md.
goja was chosen over a JS subprocess because a subprocess would silently not work in a shipped binary with no JS runtime; goja output was measured byte-identical to real Bun/faker across all 24 generators.
The M4 goja bridge commits (55229696..ca8b2e02) were discarded because M5 only needs masking, not realistic fake-value replacement, so a realistic value generator has no consumer in this chapter.

### v1.7 M5 Anonymization filters: per-column PII rules, format-preserving masking, applied over MCP and in the normal data viewer
M5 adds anonymization filters with per-column PII rules (which table.column pairs are PII), configurable per connection.
M5 anonymization uses masking only, with no Faker-based realistic-value replacement (M4 was descoped for this reason).
Hashing alone was ruled out for anonymization because a low-entropy value like a first name is guessable even when hashed and salted; masking genuinely deletes characters instead of transforming them.
Masking is deterministic per real value within a connection (same input always gives the same masked output), so correlation and referential joins across a foreign key survive without exposing or allowing recovery of the real value.
Masking shape differs per PII class (name, email, numeric/date field each need a different shape); M5's planning pass designs the actual schemes.
MCP query results pass through the configured per-column filter before being returned to an AI client.
The normal data viewer (views/grid/DataView.vue grid and the celleditor/ cell editor) gets a toggle to view the masked transform live over real data; it is a display-layer transform and never mutates the underlying row.
The planned 'id' masking kind (full redact + tag for identifier-shaped columns) was dropped because masking is opt-in per column, so an identifier nobody marks sensitive is already untouched.
M5 ships five masking kinds (name, email, text, number, date) plus redact.
M5 no longer depends on M4 (descoped); it needs M2's connection model and M1's query/result path, both already landed.
The M5 filter-rule UI is likely a table within M2's MCP tab, with storage as a new table/migration; the concrete design is left to the phase's planning pass.

### v1.7 M6 Code review, round 1
v1.7 M6 code review round 1 uses three parallel Opus subagents, one per dimension: architecture/security, functional correctness, performance/resource efficiency. They produce findings only, per CLAUDE.md's process.
v1.7 M6 review scope is wider than a normal round, by explicit instruction: the chapter's own diff (M1ab, M2-M5) is the primary target, plus a dedicated pass over v1.6's diff (origin/main..v1.6's tip, P60-P70's changes), plus anything else reviewers notice in adjacent code.
After the three review subagents report, one sequential Sonnet subagent fixes every finding judged real.
The v1.7 M6 review runs only after M1ab and M2-M5 land, because a review needs a finished tree. The v1.6-focused half needs v1.6 fully landed, which is already true.

### v1.7 M7 Code review, round 2
Code review round 2 (M7) reruns the same three-dimension review cycle in full against the tree left after M6's fixes, with the same wider v1.6-focused scope, re-reading fresh rather than trusting M6's summary.
Repeat the whole review loop in full after fixes land, per CLAUDE.md.
A review round that finds nothing real must say so rather than manufacture a finding.

### v1.7 M8 Update main docs
The v1.7 M8 milestone updates README.md, docs/ARCHITECTURE.md, docs/DEV_ENVIRONMENT.md and CLAUDE.md to cover the chapter's new subsystem: a second MCP server, its permissions/anonymization/Faker model, and its Settings-dialog toggle.
Update docs by reading the current tree, not trusting existing prose, the same bar v1.6's P70 used.
The v1.7 M8 docs update is the chapter's last milestone and must wait until both review rounds' fixes have landed.
